Electric boat maker Zin Boats raises $5M and launches propulsion business beyond the water

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Chronicling the Seattle and Pacific Northwest startup scene. SEE MORE

by Kurt Schlosser on Nov 24, 2026 at 9:58 amNovember 24, 2026 at 9:58 am

Zin Boats made a splash with fast, pricey electric boats. But most of its business is now coming from what’s under the floor.

The Seattle startup has launched Zin Power, a new division that will supply its electric propulsion systems to other boat builders and to industries that rely on diesel engines, such as construction and mining.

The company also raised $5 million to scale up production and expanded its headquarters in the Interbay neighborhood to 11,000 square feet.

About 90% of the company’s revenue comes from selling motors and batteries to customers in the marine and drone industries, founder and CEO Piotr Zin told GeekWire. A typical setup includes two motors and about 500 kilowatt-hours of battery storage.

“We noticed a steady demand for pleasure craft year over year, but at the same time a massive increase in propulsion sales,” Zin said.

Customers include StanCraft Wooden Boats, which teamed with Zin on the 340 Hammerhead E, an all-electric wooden runabout. StanCraft delivered the first one to a customer this summer.

GeekWire first checked out the company in early 2024, when it was relaunching after a pandemic shutdown with its Z2R, a 20-foot carbon fiber runabout. Later that year Zin showed off the Z11, a 37-foot electric tender designed to shuttle passengers to and from megayachts at more than 60 mph. The company said boats on the platform would cost $1.75 million to $3.75 million.

The company also offers the Z7 Convertible, a 22-foot runabout with a removable roof that it pitches for fishing, surfing and cruising. The boat has a top speed of 40 mph and a range of more than 80 miles

Zin Power grew out of the drivetrains engineered for those boats. Zin sees the marine track record as a selling point for other industries that need high torque, including construction, mining and remotely operated vehicles (ROVs).

“The ocean is one of the harshest environments there is, so the technology has already proven itself under about the toughest conditions it could face,” Zin said.

Many of those machines still run on diesel engines, a technology more than a century old. Zin said operators are eager to switch to electric.

“What’s interesting is that economics is driving the shift, not regulation or image,” he said.

Electric systems cost up to 70% less to operate, according to Zin. He estimated that replacing a single 250-horsepower industrial diesel engine that runs eight hours a day could save as much as $1.5 million over 10 years.

The new funding will pay for more manufacturing capacity, a dedicated sales and engineering team to work with boat builders, and support for customers converting from diesel. Zin calls it a seed round, with a larger raise planned down the road. The company has raised $6.5 million to date, including a $1.275 million seed round in early 2024.

Zin Boats has nine full-time employees and brings on about a dozen contractors as needed.

For now, each Zin Power system is built to order. The company’s motors range from 100 to 600 kilowatts and deliver up to 2,700 pound-feet of torque, and its systems can run fully electric or as hybrids. A proprietary control unit that Zin Power calls the “Magic Box” manages everything from charging and cooling to software and safety.

“Our goal is to keep delivering what we’ve built while developing simpler, more advanced plug-and-play systems,” Zin said. “Eventually, we want a customer to be able to pull out a diesel engine and drop in a Zin Power system as a direct one-for-one replacement.”

Tidal is banning AI-generated music entirely

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The company expects to purge 29 million tracks.

Tidal will soon become the first streaming service to take a decisive stance against AI-generated music. On Tuesday, the company announced it would begin removing wholly genAI tracks from its catalog come December 9. According to platform lead Robert Anderson, Tidal's decision will result in the deletion of approximately 29 million tracks, and set the service on the path to reject 80,000 to 85,000 songs daily. "We believe this will resonate with both artists and listeners alike, as 96 percent of all AI-generated songs receive zero plays at all," Anderson wrote on X. "We are the first DSP to do so, and we encourage our peers to follow suit."

The move comes after Tidal announced earlier this year it would demonetize all fully AI-generated track, and label every such song as either AI-generated or AI-assisted. On Tuesday, however, Anderson said even that policy hasn't been enough to combat the deluge of AI tracks that has been flooding Tidal in recent months. Over the last 90 days, 43 percent of all tracks uploaded to the service were flagged as AI-generated, according to the company.

A few months ago, @Tidal tagged and demonetized all wholly AI-generated tracks to protect the authenticity of real artists. But that's enough, as 43% of tracks distributed to us in the last 90 days have been detected as AI-generated. Something more drastic needs to be done...

That Tidal should be the first streaming service to ban AI content is somewhat surprising. Block, the company that has owned Tidal since 2021, is led by AI enthusiast Jack Dorsey. At the start of the year, Block made headlines when it laid off 4,000 of its 10,000 employees, with Dorsey attributing the decision entirely to AI. "The core thesis is simple. Intelligence tools have changed what it means to build and run a company," he wrote at the time. "A significantly smaller team, using the tools we're building, can do more and do it better."

The only other streaming platform besides Tidal to outright ban music "generated wholly or in substantial part by AI" is Bandcamp. Spotify, for instance, has tried to dance around the issue; in October, the company said it would continue to remove "slop and spam" from people's recommendations, but it did this shortly after announcing it would also allow people to remix and cover songs using AI. Soundcloud got into bed with AI music creation companies Elevenlabs and Moises; its current policies try to balance the removal of spam with allowing AI tools to proliferate. Deezer added a toggle to hide (but not delete) AI-made music, while Apple Music merely labels songs made with AI.

Arm strongarms site that hosts ad-free documentation and it may disappear

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Easy-to-use, popular, not collecting data for us, or serving our ads? Down with you!

Arm is swinging the hammer at an enthusiast’s site hosting a bloat-free version of its instruction reference documentation, demanding it be taken offline after several years of availability.

The demand from Arm lawyers to Jon Palmisciano concern Jon’s Arm Reference, a site that provides the same arm Instruction Set Architecture (ISA) info available on Arm’s site, but with what Palmisciano describes as “without a million popups and all of the bloat” that Arm provides on its own.

According to Palmisciano, his copy of the Arm ISA was culled from machine-readable XML files and stripped of the bloat. He built his version in 2022 with new CSS on top of it, and the addition of a quick search feature that, according to his comments on Hacker News, isn’t available in the official version.

“The aim has always been to make it the most comfortable and bullshit-free browsing experience for people who need these docs daily,” Palmisciano wrote in HN. Based on the responses the site seems to have plenty of fans.

That doesn’t matter to Arm, though: As far as the lawyers are concerned, Palmisciano’s site is a copyright violation due to its use of Arm trademarks and inclusion of copyrighted reference material for the Armv9-A and Armv9-A architecture.

Arm doesn’t care if Palmisciano’s project is well intentioned or for the benefit of the many software developers that write code for its chipsets, either: They want it offline by December 4 – 10 days from the publication of this story – or else.

As for what is going to happen, it seems like Jon’s Arm Reference will likely cease to exist in a little over a week – at least in its current form.

“Ideally I'd love to just get permission from Arm to keep the site up, but I'm pretty pessimistic about that,” Promisciano told us. That said, Promisciano has “already seen a few archives and mirrors already.” The HN thread discussing the takedown order includes links to several versions, and Promisciano has another trick up his sleeve that he’s considering playing: Open-sourcing the software he used to build his ISA page.

“I'd also consider open-sourcing my tooling so that people can (for their own private use) turn the original XML downloads from Arm into an identical site they can host locally,” Promisciano told us. He said that he'll likely tweet about opening the tooling if he does do so. He's also considered directing the URL for Jon's Arm reference to point to the open-source tool, but Arm wants the subdomain it's hosted on removed from his site as well, so that may not happen.

Arm has been in touch to defend itself as well, saying that Arm appreciates the efforts of people like Promisciano, but that it still has to protect its brand, and documentation.

"We have a responsibility to ensure Arm is the primary source of technical resources and information and to protect the Arm brand," an Arm spokesperson told us. "Striking the right balance between third parties sharing helpful peer-to-peer resources while ensuring copyrighted Arm-related technical content is accurate has been critical to building the thriving ecosystem we have today."

In other words, if you’re an Arm dev worried about losing a valuable resource and having to turn to a website that bombards you with junk just to look up a few simple opcodes and operands you’d better find a backup host before next month. That said, Arm is willing to work something out with Promisciano, the company told us. "We have recently flagged our concerns with regard to the use of Arm copyright content and the Arm brand on this website and shared the Arm trademark guidelines," Arm explained. "Our hope is to open a constructive dialogue, and we've asked that the website owner reach out as we seek to resolve this."As Promisciano posted on HN, Arm is asking for removal of its trademark and copyrighted documentation. That, Promisciano said, "seems hard to work around while still keeping the site useful."  ®

New York Just Banned Subscriptions For Features Your Car Already Has

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

In-vehicle subscriptions are pretty universally maligned among car owners. Most folks don't like the feeling of having to pay every month for features their cars were supposed to come with, and now New York State is doing something about it. Governor Kathy Hochul just signed a bill that bars automakers, dealers and their agents from selling subscriptions for features that are already installed.

The new law, which takes effect on February 27, 2027, is part of a new section of the state's General Business Law, according to Spectrum News. You can read the law for yourself here. It lays out fairly simple ground rules when it comes to subscriptions dealers or manufacturers can offer you: The feature must use hardware already in the car when it was bought or leased, and it must keep working without continual costs or support from the automaker or a third party. Those two things pretty much bring an end to crap like subscription-based heated seats and alike. It also is supposed to cover hands-off driving systems and enhanced navigation. Basically, once the hardware is physically in the car and the manufacturer isn't paying anything for you to use it, they can't then charge you. OEMs will also not be able to charge for software updates that fix safety defects.

Hochul called the practice a "deceptive business model," according to CBS 6 Albany. She also told automakers to keep their hands out of New Yorkers' wallets. It passed through the New York State Senate 57-3.

As with all good things, there are some exceptions. First of all, your lease or payment plan doesn't count as a subscription, so don't even try it, slick.

The law also doesn't apply to navigation updates, infotainment features, satellite radio, in-vehicle WiFi, telematics, roadside assistance or "vehicle-connected services" that rely on cellular data or other networks, according to Motor1. It also exempts software-dependent driver assistance features, like Tesla's Full Self-Driving or GM's Super Cruise.

I know what you're thinking. Didn't I just mention that hands-free driving systems are covered? Well, yes, but subscriptions tied to software and data are likely to survive, while hardware-only features are the real targets. Automakers will almost certainly make the argument that things like FSD and Super Cruise are "connected" or "software-dependent."

One prime example of what this bill aims to get rid of is BMW keeping things like a 360-degree camera behind a paywall. That hardware is already built into the car, and, as we reported, it doesn't require a data connection to operate. It's the same thing with features like high-beam assist and remote start, so I imagine it'll be hard for BMW and other companies with similar schemes to argue that these are "connected." I mean, these are the same people who argue that buyers actually want subscriptions, which is certainly not true.

In any case, violators will face civil penalties of up to $250 per point-of-sale for each violation, though it's not clear exactly how that'll be counted. It's not clear if other states are going to follow suit, but here's hoping.

TIDAL will delete 29 million AI-generated songs from its catalogue

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The streaming service says labels and cut royalties were not enough, as AI tracks made up 43% of recent uploads.

TIDAL will begin deleting all wholly AI-generated tracks on December 9, about 29 million songs, and expects to reject 80,000 to 85,000 more a day. It had already labelled and demonetised AI music in July, but says 43% of tracks it received in the past 90 days were AI-made.

TIDAL will start deleting every wholly AI-generated track from its catalogue on December 9, removing about 29 million songs. Robert Andersen, who leads TIDAL at parent company Block, announced the plan on X on Tuesday. TIDAL’s own account confirmed the date.

Andersen said the songs should be gone by the start of 2027. TIDAL also expects to turn away 80,000 to 85,000 AI-made tracks a day. He called it the first streaming service to take this step and urged rivals to follow.

In July, TIDAL began labelling AI tracks and cutting off their royalties. Andersen said that had not been enough.

“43% of tracks distributed to us in the last 90 days have been detected as AI-generated. Something more drastic needs to be done,” Andersen wrote.

Most of that music finds no audience. Andersen said 96% of AI-generated songs on TIDAL get zero plays.

Rivals face the same flood. More than half the music uploaded to Deezer in June was made by AI.

Others have stopped short of deleting it. Spotify has pulled 50M spam tracks from its recommendations, but left them on the service. Apple Music has made AI tags mandatory for record companies.

The cut covers only songs made wholly by AI, which TIDAL finds with detection tools. Its terms of service warn those tools can wrongly tag tracks, and users can ask support to fix a label.

TinyBuild CEO Downplays Threats of AI Game-Cloning

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Daniel is a lifelong gamer with a love for various genres and gameplay styles who enjoys exploring games and playing from various angles.

As part of the ever-growing ingress of AI slop into the gaming industry, a new threat has recently arisen in the form of AI game-cloning. In a nutshell, a user feeds footage and descriptions of a game into their toaster of choice, as well as more specific data like coding cribbed from released demos, and instructs it to make effectively the same game. The infamous recent example of this was Needle in a Haystack Simulator, a small indie game that, following its announcement, received a deluge of AI-made knock-offs that flooded the Steam store.

Developers are taking this threat very seriously. During the most recent Steam Next Fest, which was already plagued by AI slop, several indie devs decided against releasing demos out of concern that bad actors would churn out knock-offs before they could release their actual games. Some developers have admitted to receiving anonymous extortion threats, saying that if a ransom isn’t paid, the crooks will release an AI knock-off of their game before they can release the real one.

There’s even been rumors that the PC release of Grand Theft Auto 6 could be expedited just to get ahead of the proverbial slop factory that’s already churning out identical open-world crime sandbox games.

Obviously, this is a big problem, and it’s only going to get worse as AI gets smarter, but at least according to game publisher tinyBuild’s CEO Alex Nichiporchik, developers should not be afraid of these malicious duplicates. As he explained in an interview with Gamesindustry.biz, AI may be able to imitate a game’s basic aesthetics, but game feel is another matter.

If you're looking for interesting new games to keep an eye on, check out the Coolest Demos from Steam Next Fest October 2026.

As Nichiporchik points out, while AI-made knock-offs of existing or announced games may have some superficial similarities, such as setting, basic game concepts, or story beats, those elements are only part of what makes a particular game a unique experience. In fact, it’s the “experience” itself that serves as the primary differentiator, and despite its best efforts, AI can’t do much in that department.

“There's a reason that every kind of AI slop game feels the same," he explained. "It's because when you try to make something from zero, AI will multiply your skills, and zero times a thousand is still zero.”

There's a reason that every kind of AI slop game feels the same.

Nichiporchik went on to say that, rather than being afraid of having their ideas stolen, developers should consider what they can do to make their games more difficult to duplicate, primarily in how the game handles and in its core gameplay loop.

As Nichiporchik himself puts it, you could have an entire army of designers and researchers trying to completely duplicate something popular, but it just won’t have that same distinctive zing as the real thing, authentically brought to life by its original creators. It’s not unlike the number of bodies games like Fortnite or Grand Theft Auto have left in their wake as other studios try unsuccessfully to ape their formulas.

“As long as you have great game feel and a great game loop, I think you're going to be OK,” Nichiporchik assured.

Despite his assurances, Nichiporchik admitted that these AI-made games, not unlike fast food, are ubiquitous and rather obnoxious. Part of the problem is that AI knock-offs are basically trying to capitalize on the hype generated by a game’s announcement, as well as promotion like trailers or demos, stealing attention away from the genuine article or even fooling players not in the know into buying the wrong product.

Nichiporchik’s suggestion for this is to narrow the gap between announcement and release. While the standard strategy is to give the hype thrusters time to warm up and let the game accumulate wishlists and word-of-mouth, playing things close to the vest with a faster turnaround seems to work just as well.

As Nichiporchik explained, the most recent game published by tinyBuild, Probably Stolen - Cyberpunk Shopkeeper Sim, was announcement-ready as early as May, but tinyBuild and Questing Goose Studio decided to wait until August to announce, then release in October instead. That game’s done gangbusters despite somewhat repetitive gameplay, so it seems like the strategy worked out, and without giving bad actors nearly as much time to try to copy their notes.

Hideki Hayakawa believes that AI will never replace the human touch of creation for the video game industry.

University of Phoenix assessment leaders present hands-on framework for building customized AI assessment assistants at national conference

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Workshop at Indiana University's Assessment Institute demonstrates practical approaches to designing AI-powered tools for learning assessment

PHOENIX, Nov. 24, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Gretchen Meyers, Ed.D., and Melinda Kulick, Ed.D., leaders in the University of Phoenix Assessment and Institutional Research team, recently presented a hands-on workshop demonstrating how assessment professionals can design and build customized artificial intelligence (AI) assessment assistants at the Indiana University Assessment Institute, one of the nation's leading conferences focused on assessment and improvement in higher education.

The workshop, "From Generic Artificial Intelligence (AI) to Custom Assessment Assistant: A Hands-On Session," guided attendees through the process of developing AI-powered assessment assistants designed for specialized assessment-related tasks and workflows. Participants explored the differences between general prompting techniques and custom task-based AI configuration, gaining practical experience through a live design and guided-build session led by Kulick and Meyers.

Designing AI tools for assessment professionals

As higher education institutions explore new applications for artificial intelligence, assessment leaders are examining how AI tools can be configured to support precise processes while maintaining alignment with institutional goals, learning outcomes and sound assessment practices.

"Assessment professionals have an opportunity to move beyond using AI as a general-purpose tool and begin designing solutions that address specific assessment needs," said Meyers, director of Assessment at University of Phoenix. "Our goal was to help participants better understand the design process and provide practical experience creating AI assistants that can support meaningful assessment work."

The workshop focused on helping participants understand how customized AI applications can be built around clearly defined tasks, creating tools designed to support assessment activities rather than relying solely on broad, open-ended prompting.

Advancing practical applications of AI in higher education assessment

The presentation builds on University of Phoenix thought leadership and research examining the role of generative AI in learning assessment and academic innovation. Earlier work by University assessment leaders including Meyers introduced a framework for integrating generative AI into assessment design grounded in established learning theory and authentic assessment practices.

The University's assessment leaders have previously shared research and practical guidance on AI-supported assessment design through scholarly publications and national conference presentations focused on helping educators understand the opportunities and considerations associated with AI integration.

"Many assessment professionals are interested in understanding how AI can support their work in practical and responsible ways," said Kulick, manager of Assessment at University of Phoenix. "By allowing participants to experience the design and development process firsthand, we were able to focus on application, experimentation and problem-solving rather than theory alone."

Contributing to national conversations about AI and student learning

The workshop aligns with broader University of Phoenix efforts to explore the role of artificial intelligence across curriculum design, teaching and learning, assessment, and institutional processes. Recent University research, publications and thought leadership have examined responsible AI adoption, workforce-related AI skills, and frameworks for integrating AI across higher education environments.

Hosted by Indiana University Indianapolis, the Assessment Institute serves as a national forum for higher education professionals to share innovative practices, explore emerging issues and advance evidence-informed approaches to student learning and institutional improvement.

Tidal labeled them (a step many other companies have also been taking) and removed them from its royalty pool. GTA 6 is giving Carly Rae Jepsens Run Away With Me a second life

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Grand Theft Auto VI is a massive pop culture event that is affecting a whole lot of people from a whole lot of different angles. Arguably, few are benefiting more from the game's launch last week than Carly Rae Jepsen.

The Canadian pop star broke big worldwide with "Call Me Maybe" in 2012 and has followed up with an extremely respectable career that includes a devoted fanbase and eight studio albums, including the recently released Day & Night. However, one of her most beloved (and slightly less-well-known) songs is blowing up on streaming for the first time in a decade thanks to its inclusion in GTA 6, per Billboard Canada.

That would be "Run Away With Me," the opening track to Jepsen's highly acclaimed 2015 record Emotion. Aside from being featured on GTA 6's in-game radio station Flash FM, "Run Away With Me" also soundtracks an important early-game mission. According to Billboard and an X account called Carly Rae Jepsen Charts, the song was streamed more than 100,000 times on both Saturday and Sunday, its two biggest individual streaming days since the song was new.

To be clear, this is not necessarily a case of GTA breathing life into an obscure old song. "Run Away With Me" made a small splash on music charts in Canada and a handful of other territories back in 2015, and was a critical darling both at the time and now. But the GTA bump is clearly very real, nonetheless.

Warning: The following section contains spoilers for an early mission in GTA 6.

If you're curious as to the specific context in which "Run Away With Me" and its iconic distorted saxophone riff are used in GTA 6, allow me to explain. During the game's first story chapter, players will run into a mission called "Day Release" that involves protagonists Jason and Lucia going to a party on an expensive yacht. Lucia's parole ankle monitor is temporarily disabled for this mission, and the whole idea is that she and Jason get to have a fun day of rich people debauchery together for the first time since she got out of prison.

A few minutes into the mission, Jason and Lucia are invited to ride jet skis with the host of the yacht party, and this is where the "Run Away With Me" needle-drop happens. As the two lovers speed around the waters of a fictional Miami stand-in, Jepsen's catchy chorus reverberates throughout the action. The best part is that the mission keeps going and includes several little gameplay vignettes, most of which are scored by a longer remix of the song provided by Overmono and Pegassi.

What makes this great for those who enjoy both GTA and Carly Rae Jepsen is that the song isn't used just because it's good. It also perfectly evokes the excitement of the early stages of being in love and sonically fits the high-speed action during the mission.

Needless to say, it's been stuck in my head ever since I played the mission a few days ago. I hope it gets there for you, too. Uncommon Thinkers: Saf Rabah wants to make it easier to build housing, one permit at a time

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

by Grace Kaste on Nov 24, 2026 at 9:50 amNovember 24, 2026 at 9:53 am

Editor’s note: This series profiles five Seattle-area “Uncommon Thinkers,” who will be recognized Dec. 10 at the GeekWire Gala in partnership with Greater Seattle Partners.

When startup founder Safouen (Saf) Rabah decided to get in on the AI boom, he didn’t start by coding or by training any models. He got in touch with urban planners at Bellevue City Hall, and together they combed through nearly 300 land use permit applications to find common mistakes and bottlenecks.

Bellevue began using Rabah’s platform, Govstream.AI, in the summer of 2025, starting with an AI assistant for city staff. In a test using past applications, about three times as many were judged complete with Govstream’s tool. Rabah says that change alone has the potential to alter the housing stock of the city.

“Why can’t we build more housing? All the macro economic reasons, inflation, material costs, labor, land scarcity, are all exacerbated by the slow permitting process,” Rabah said. “That’s what we want to change.”

So, how did Rabah, a B2B software engineer by training, find his way to land use permitting?

Rabah hadn’t heard of the government technology sector, aka GovTech, until mid-career, but looking back, the signs were all there. He was born in Sfax, Tunisia, and grew up in Tunis. Throughout his childhood, he dreamed of running for office.

“I come from a small developing country, so there’s always this idea of, what could a small developing country become? How can it prosper and grow?” Rabah said. “Building has always been a big part of what I imagined myself doing.”

As a teenager in the 1980s, he attended a USAID-funded boarding school in Tunis. The school was part of Cold War efforts to expand American soft power around the Middle East; classes were conducted in English and many of the students went on to university in North America.

“The school had the first Macintosh lab in all of Africa,” Rabah said. “I can still picture the USAID logo on the back of each computer.”

Rabah got a scholarship to University of British Columbia, where he studied electrical engineering while running what he calls his “first entrepreneurial venture”: a service that built and hosted websites for small businesses.

That led to a job at a startup called Pivotal, a customer relationship management platform where Rabah led a team responsible for e-business applications. After Pivotal went public, Rabah left for a job at TELUS, one of Canada’s “big three” telecommunications corporations, but quickly felt frustrated by the dynamics at such a large company.

“Even though I worked on nascent products, it was very, very bureaucratic,” Rabah said. “There were committees, a hundred thousand employees. I missed building things.”

Rabah began to look for a new job that would not only allow him to build but also to make a difference.

“Being an immigrant, it’s always tempting to stay content with having a good job, good status,” Rabah said. “But I’ve always enjoyed the ‘what if?’ mindset, the clean slate. It’s this way today, but could we imagine it to be something different?”

That vision led him to Socrata, which in 2010 was just a nascent government technology startup.

“I really connected with the founder,” Rabah said. “It had a really cool mission, it combined the ‘do-well’ with ‘do-good.’”

Socrata was one of the first platforms to host data portals for local governments and federal agencies, allowing citizens to access and map public data sets. Many U.S. cities, including Seattle, still use Socrata’s software today to publish information including crime logs, permits, and budgets.

“We were a big part of pioneering the open data movement,” Rabah said. “At one point almost every major city was our customer, and half the states.”

Socrata was acquired by Tyler Technologies in 2018, and Rabah stayed on until 2024.

“The funny part is today we use that data quite a lot in our work because one of the most popular data sets that any city publishes is its permit data,” Rabah said. “We’ve even built a connector between Socrata and Govstream.”

But once Socrata was acquired, Rabah began to feel the same way he had at TELUS: boxed in by bureaucracy.

“Tyler was a really good company, but it is a big company,” Rabah said. “Tech was getting repetitive, and I was thinking about leaving it altogether.”

“The thing that pushed me over the edge is that AI happened,” Rabah said. “It was one of those moments where once you start working with it, you really can’t help but think, ‘I can revisit virtually every assumption I’ve made over the last 20 years, every constraint.’”

Immediately, he thought of his passion for building—building a startup, building a better country, and literally building a building.

“I’ve been fascinated by the built environment since my Lego days: What makes some cities livable and others not?” Rabah said. “Then, living in Seattle, you see the lack of housing, affordability, and the homelessness problem we have, and you’re like, why can’t we build more housing? I started believing that these problems are in fact solvable if we simply speed things up and simplify things with AI.”

In July of 2024, Rabah began working with his “design partner,” Bellevue’s Development Services Department, to learn more about common permitting problems.

“We started with something visionary and high-level, but you can only get there if you understand the granular problems of the day-to-day,” Rabah said. “So we talked with developers and builders and we talked with the people on the other side of the counter who are processing these permits and guiding these builders.”

He learned that most permitting mistakes are avoidable, like applying for the wrong zone or forgetting to include a north arrow on an architectural plan. Those oversights can send an application back to square one while clogging up the permitting process for all other projects.

“The wait time disproportionately affects lower margin projects,” Rabah said. “It really constrains the supply of housing, especially the housing we need more of, which is the more affordable kind.”

“I’ve always enjoyed the ‘what if?’ mindset, the clean slate. It’s this way today, but could we imagine it to be something different?”

Govstream’s AI assistants are designed to help first-time builders avoid those errors and make permitting faster for everyone. Rabah and his team began by building what they call an “intelligent intake” platform for a city permitting portal, which guided users through preparing to submit their applications.

Bellevue tested Govstream’s intake tool on 276 past residential building permit applications. The city reported a completeness rate of 67% in the test, compared with 22% when those applications were originally filed.

More recently, they’ve begun to build out an AI assistant for the backend, which assists city permit techs with the initial review and reduces what Rabah calls “toil,” like answering unnecessary pre-application emails and tracking down missing documents.

Bellevue staff estimated that Govstream’s tools saved them 152 hours over one month this summer by helping them research and respond to pre-application inquiries.

“For a developer, time is money, so the type of housing that can be built in Bellevue will become more affordable,” said Jake Hesselgesser, Bellevue’s development services director. “This technology has a direct and material impact on the cost of housing.”

Eventually, Govstream.AI will be an end-to-end service, assisting in later stages of the permitting process, too. But Rabah is emphatic that Govstream will not give AI the final say.

“We always put our AI systems in the role of assistants that suggest stuff, but never decide stuff,” Rabah said.

Rabah says Govstream uses preset rules, outside the AI model, to check whether a portion of the application is complete, whether a site needs a geotechnical report, or whether an architect is properly licensed. In Bellevue, city staff retain final authority.

Hesselgesser said that Rabah’s conservative approach to AI is what made the city of Bellevue want to work with his startup.

“Our core principle around Bellevue’s use of AI is to retain human decision making, and we wanted to ensure that we were aligned with a company with the same core value,” Hesselgesser said. “Saf took that holistic approach.”

Around the office, Rabah has become known for his eagerness to build. According to Mariia Derevianko, head of marketing, he spends his weekends building out a product or an idea in order to workshop it with the team.

“He’s very hands-on with the product, with design, marketing, all of the aspects,” Derevianko said. “He never waits for the engineers to build something.”

Most recently, he built a lead tracker to research which cities Govstream should partner with, and previously, he vibe-coded one of the research assistants in the Govstream platform as a template for his software engineers.

Derevianko, who joined the team last year after Rabah poached her from AWS, said he’s also known for his kindness.

“I originally didn’t want to join a start-up,” Derevianko said. “I only said yes because I saw he is a genuinely good person who really cares about this project.”

Govstream.AI has already won at least 12 contracts around the Puget Sound, including King County and the City of Everett, as well as cities across the country, such as Louisville, Kentucky.

Ultimately, Rabah has his eyes set on Seattle, where construction and development has been on the decline. Last December, the city council passed permanent “middle housing” rules to upzone residential neighborhoods and encourage the construction of ADUs, all of which will need a permit.

Seattle tested a competing AI tool from CivCheck in 2025. In June, city staff recommended a pilot that would let applicants check their submissions for completeness before filing.

“We need affordable housing in Seattle,” Rabah said. “We would really, really love to help our home city streamline permitting and remove it as a barrier.”

In founding Govstream.AI, Rabah has found the job he has been looking for: one in which he can build things without the bureaucracy of a large company, all while “doing good.”

“This has become my obsession,” Rabah said. “I can point to things in a city and say, maybe Govstream was a part of what allowed this project to get built.”

Our definition of business isn’t exactly common.

Greater Seattle Partners is the region’s public-private economic development organization uniting business, government, and community leaders to promote Greater Seattle on the global stage. We elevate the region’s story, attract international investment, and build regional economic collaboration to advance inclusive and sustainable economic growth.

Does Working Harder Make Learning Fun?

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

A new psychology study challenges the classic educational advice that simply putting in more effort makes learning enjoyable. Three experiments reveal that while cognitive effort improves comprehension, it does not spark curiosity or enjoyment, instead, preexisting interest motivates students to engage in the first place.

Parents and educators frequently repeat a familiar piece of advice when children groan over difficult homework: if you just try harder, you’ll start to enjoy it. Behind this common wisdom lies an intuitive assumption that sustained cognitive effort naturally blossoms into curiosity and intrinsic motivation.

Yet a new psychological investigation from Kyushu University suggests that this relationship works in the exact opposite direction.

Published in Acta Psychologica, the study demonstrates that forcing effort improves basic comprehension, but it does not make dry or unappealing material more interesting. Instead, genuine interest and liking come first—serving as the psychological spark that motivates learners to invest cognitive effort voluntarily.

“I’m curious if a student is made to work harder, does that bring the same benefits as effort they choose to give,” asked Yimeng Jin, a doctoral student at Kyushu University’s Graduate School of Systems Life Sciences and first author of the study. “It matters because many learning environments rely on required effort, such as assignments, on the assumption that more effort is always better.”

To disentangle voluntary motivation from mandatory mental exertion, Jin and senior author Professor Johan Lauwereyns at Kyushu University’s Faculty of Arts and Science conducted three behavioral experiments involving 110 university students.

Participants read pairs of seemingly unrelated sentences and decided whether they wanted to construct an explanation connecting them. By tracking participants’ initial choices, task completion, and time spent, the team isolated two distinct components of learning: willingness to engage and cognitive effort invested.

Across the three experimental phases, a clear cognitive pattern emerged:

“Interestingly, participants who were required to explain spent more time on the task than those who chose to do so,” Jin noted. “Perhaps they initially skipped it because the sentences were harder to understand, so they needed more time to make sense of them. It’s a bit like struggling through homework in a subject you don’t enjoy. Together, the experiments suggest that effort can help people make sense of information, while interest is more closely linked to their willingness to engage.”

While the researchers caution that short laboratory reading tasks among university students differ from long-term classroom learning in younger children, the findings carry broad implications for how educators, parents, and communicators design learning environments. Rather than relying solely on mandatory drill work to manufacture enthusiasm, sparking authentic curiosity first may unlock deeper, faster comprehension.

“The findings have implications far outside education. The connection between engagement and effort is key whenever our understanding depends on reading texts,” concluded Lauwereyns.

Does working harder on a subject automatically make it more enjoyable?

No. Researchers at Kyushu University found that while putting in cognitive effort helps students better understand difficult material, it does not increase their interest in or enjoyment of the subject.

What actually motivates students to put effort into learning?

The experiments showed that preexisting interest and liking come first. When students already find a topic intriguing, they are far more willing to engage voluntarily, and they comprehend the material more efficiently than when forced to work through topics they want to skip.

What happens when students are forced to work on material they don’t find interesting?

When participants were required to explain material they initially chose to skip, they spent significantly more time struggling with the task, gained less comprehension compared to voluntary effort, and showed no increase in enjoyment.

Effort enhances understanding, while appreciation drives engagement in Cross-sentence semantic processing

Comprehensive reading requires active semantic processing, going beyond explicitly stated information to construct connections and meaning. Building on discourse coherence theory, Reader-Response Theory and insights from Language Poetry, the present research investigates how active engagement and cognitive effort shape semantic processing.

We propose that evaluative dimensions central to aesthetic appreciation, namely understanding, interest, and liking, also play important roles in semantic processing.

To examine these processes, we developed a novel cross-sentence elaboration paradigm using unconnected, randomly paired sentences that create semantic gaps and encourage reader-driven meaning construction.

In Experiment 1, participants could voluntarily invest effort by thinking aloud during elaboration. Results showed greater volitional engagement was associated with higher levels of understanding, interest, and appreciation.

Experiment 2 dissociated willingness to engage from actual effort, revealing that appreciation predicted participants’ willingness to invest further effort, whereas additional completed effort did not consistently increase appreciation.

Experiment 3 examined forced effort, showing that externally imposed effort enhanced semantic understanding but did not consistently increase appreciation, highlighting the distinct roles of volitional and imposed effort.

An integrated 2 × 2 analysis of Experiments 2 and 3 showed that active engagement and cognitive effort made distinct, interactive contributions to evaluation and processing time, with completed cognitive effort more reliably predicting accurate recognition memory and active engagement more closely tied to memory for the processing context.

Together, these findings clarify the interplay between engagement, effort, understanding, and appreciation, offering new insights into the cognitive dynamics of active meaning construction in semantic processing.

Anderson University Receives $5 Million Grant From Lilly Endowment to Advance Use of Artificial Intelligence

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

ANDERSON, Ind., Nov. 24, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- Anderson University (AU) has received a grant of $5 million from Lilly Endowment Inc. to transform AU's use of Artificial Intelligence (AI).

"We are deeply grateful to the Lilly Endowment for its generous $5 million grant supporting the Artificial Intelligence in Higher Education initiative," said Scott Moats, president of Anderson University. "This investment will help Anderson University thoughtfully advance the use of artificial intelligence in higher education. I also want to thank the faculty and staff whose work made this grant possible. Special thanks to Provost Courtney Taylor for leading the grant team and bringing this effort together."

Current internal data reveals that while 78% of faculty and staff already use AI to some degree, over half lack the confidence or training to use it effectively. Moreover, 82% of AU alumni report that AI is important to their current careers, and 83% of current students express concern that over-reliance on these tools could reduce learning.

"The integration of Artificial Intelligence and generative AI does not diminish the purpose of Christian higher education or undermine its goals," Taylor said. "This technology offers institutions of higher education an opportunity to innovate in our approach to teaching and learning. Anderson University will use AI as a catalyst to strengthen human formation, redesign learning and build institutional coherence so that faculty, students, and staff can engage AI ethically."

With the $5 million of support from Lilly Endowment, Anderson University will implement an institution-wide transformation focused on four mutually reinforcing pillars over a three-year period from Jan.1, 2027 through Dec. 31, 2029.

Curriculum & Assessment Redesign: Transitioning from "AI-proof" assignments to AI-resilient assessment models. This includes integrating AI-informed learning modules into general education and cornerstone courses to emphasize critical reasoning and ethical evaluation.

Faculty & Staff Training and Professional Development: Establishing an AI Fellows program to lead peer-to-peer development. The project will provide enterprise-level access to AI tools and robust training to move the campus from isolated experimentation to a shared institutional standard.

Institutional Framework and Governance: Developing a cross-functional AI Leadership Council to provide clear, evergreen guidance on data privacy (including FERPA), academic integrity, and mission-aligned usage grounded in Christian theological commitments.

Operational AI Integration: Launching pilot programs in Admissions, Marketing, Advancement, and the Registrar's Office to automate administrative tasks, thereby reclaiming time for high-value, relational, human-centered work.

Anderson University's AI projects are being funded through Lilly Endowment's initiative, Artificial Intelligence in Higher Education. This initiative is designed to support colleges and universities in Indiana as they develop and implement approaches for using artificial intelligence (AI). The grants will help ensure that their students, faculty and staff are well-equipped to learn, work and live in a world increasingly influenced by AI. Anderson University is one of 35 colleges and universities in Indiana receiving funding through the initiative.

About Lilly Endowment Inc.Lilly Endowment Inc. is an Indianapolis-based, private foundation created in 1937 by J.K. Lilly and his sons, Eli and J.K. Jr., through gifts of stock in their pharmaceutical business, Eli Lilly and Company. While those gifts remain the financial bedrock of the Endowment, the Endowment is a separate entity from the company, with a distinct governing board, staff and location. Although the Endowment maintains a special commitment to its founders' hometown, Indianapolis, and home state, Indiana, it also funds programs throughout the United States, especially in the field of religion.

Anderson University educates students for lives of faith and service, offering more than 50 undergraduate majors, 30 three-year degrees, 18 NCAA Division III intercollegiate sports, alongside adult, graduate, and online programs. The private, liberal arts institution is fully accredited and recognized for excellence in business, computer science, cybersecurity, engineering, music, nursing, psychology, social work, and teacher education programs. Established in 1917 in Anderson, Indiana, by the Church of God, the university remains committed to its Christ-centered mission.

SenseNet lands $43M financing deal for early wildfire detection and intelligence

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Challenges, breakthroughs and big ideas in the race to protect our planet. SEE MORE

by Lisa Stiffler on Nov 24, 2026 at 9:47 amNovember 24, 2026 at 9:47 am

SenseNet, a startup providing wildfire detection and intelligence, on Tuesday announced a $43 million financing package. The Vancouver, B.C.-based company says the funding will support its expansion across the U.S. and internationally.

The startup uses a suite of technologies for the rapid detection of wildfires, including environmental sensors, AI-enabled cameras, satellite data, drone technology and predictive analytics. Its monitoring footprint covers 450 million acres in a dozen countries.

SenseNet says its technology has provided early alerts for more than 10,000 fires, in some cases hours before the first 911 calls.

“Wildfire protection is no longer just about detecting a fire as quickly as possible. It’s about understanding what’s happening, what could happen next, and giving the right people the information they need to act,” Hamed Noori, co-founder and CEO of SenseNet, said in a statement.

Noori launched SenseNet in 2019 with Chief Technology Officer Shahab Bahrami. Both hold doctorates in electrical and computer engineering from the University of British Columbia.

The Series B round was led by Idealist Capital, with participation from Fusion Fund, Stormbreaker Ventures, Thin Line Capital, National Bank of Canada and The Sitka Foundation.

The financing includes purchases of newly issued shares, which put money into SenseNet; secondary share sales, which pay existing shareholders; and debt. SenseNet did not disclose how much of the $43 million falls into each category.

The startup previously raised $12.78 million and has 30 employees, according to PitchBook.

SenseNet competes with companies including OroraTech, Dryad and Pano AI, whose cameras, software and alerting services are used by Washington’s Department of Natural Resources to detect wildfires. The companies are developing their technology as warming temperatures and drier conditions increase wildfire risks.

The Climate Pledge is a commitment to reach net-zero carbon emissions by 2040, co-founded by Amazon and Global Optimism in 2019.

Seven years later, the community has grown to over 700 signatories across 62 industries and 49 countries and territories, reducing emissions, scaling emerging climate solutions, and addressing challenges across operations and value chains.

"Blizzard, I'm looking at you" - Path of Exile 2 boss calls out lackluster game announcements

📅 Nov 24, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Grinding Gear Games knows a thing or three here

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Right now, the devs behind Path of Exile 2 have a right to be confident. They've created a legitimate rival to the Diablo series, through consistent updates and worthwhile changes, fostering a strong community in the process. During a recent presentation, one of the key devs behind the action-RPG spoke on why he believes their approach to livestreams is much better than the rest of the industry.

Jonathan Rogers, technical director at Path of Exile studio Grinding Gear Games, went into the subject while giving a talk at this year's DevGAMM. "I've seen some truly terrible livestreams for game announcements," he says. "Blizzard, I'm looking at you."

At least he's laying his thoughts out plainly. His first point is a simple one: show the game immediately. "Get to the damn gameplay," he states emphatically. "Nobody cares about your life story, nobody cares about the game's history, nobody cares about how you celebrate your audience or anything like that."

I would definitely be on his side for this much. Too often I find myself sitting through a preview event, waiting for when the actual insight into the game I'm waiting for shows up. The rest is often just fluff.

His second point is another critical one: Lead with your best stuff. "The problem I've seen in presentations is they want to have this Steve Jobs, 'One more thing' moment," Rogers explains. "Don't do that. Lead with the best that you've got, show me what that is. It's just going to get people excited. You want people hyped."

Nintendo is the obvious culprit here, to the point we're all on tenterhooks at the end of every Nintendo Direct, waiting for one last reveal or announcement. There's room to use such things sparingly, but it's a cliché by now. Give us the goods up front!

His remaining four points on this subject are: Sell the story of playing; This is a tutorial; Spoil the game; and it's OK to be long. The first refers to how the game feels to play, the second and third are self-explanatory, and the fourth I'd consider more of a spectrum.

Weekly digests, tales from the communities you love, and more

If we're here strictly for a first look reveal, brevity helps to start. Get to the meat of what you want us to see, then save the rest for a post-show stream. But if you know you have a fanbase ready to devour every fine detail, then rinse whatever you have to showcase.

Path of Exile 2 is about to go 1.0, and it has hundreds of thousands of players. This is a team that knows what they're talking about, especially when it comes to what they don't like about official Diablo and World of Warcraft streams.

Path of Exile 2 is "f***ing genius," Baldur's Gate 3 publishing lead says – early access has been so successful "it won't even matter if nobody buys anything" after 1.0's free-to-play shift

I'm pretty sure this guy could stop even Doctor Doom in his tracks

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Marvel Rivals is officially getting its first Lego set, with a Lego Jeff the Land Shark model swimming out of the screen and onto shelves at the beginning of next year.

Due to land on January 1, 2027, this model is made up of just under 500 pieces and is a mid-size figure – it's around 5 inches high and 8.5 inches long. Rather than being a static display, though, it mimics some of the best Lego sets and is much more interactive than that. The mouth, legs, tongue, and tail of Lego Jeff the Land Shark all move, letting you set up the Marvel Rivals character however you want.

Like so many Marvel kits, this one straddles the line between playset and display piece. It's rated for ages 12+ and seems sturdy enough for younger fans to have a lot of fun with, but is also highly posable so collectors can show off Jeff at his best.UK: £44.99 at Lego

If you've been following the week's Lego news, you'll know that this isn't the first we've seen of the adorable critter; the model was leaked ahead of time, and it divided fans. From initial reactions, it feels as though the community was split down the middle. Some felt it was an adorable rendition of Jeff and were happy to see it, while others really didn't like the design.

Now that we've got official promo shots, I think Lego Jeff the Land Shark comes off much better than it did in leaks. It's true that the final product doesn't capture how squidgy and rotund the character looks in-game, but that was always going to be a tall order – Lego is obviously angular by design so doesn't lend itself to soft, circular shapes unless you're building something massive. As such, I think the team has done a great job considering the scale of the model.
